## Document 006: 115年9月全國賦稅收入初步統計 (2026-10-13)

Source PDF: all report pdf/115年9月全國賦稅收入初步統計 (2026-10-13).pdf
Report source: MOF
Date: 2026-10-13
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

網際網路 https://www.mof.gov.tw同步發送 
下次發布日期：115 年 11 月 11 日下午 4 時

聯 絡 人： 陳 巧 芸 科 長 
電   話：02-2322-7555 
115年 9月全國賦稅收入初步統計 
一、全國賦稅收入 
（一）本月數 
9 月實徵淨額 4,965 億元，較上年同月增加 813 億元(+19.6%)。 
（二）累計數 
累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額 3 兆 5,714 億元，較上年同期增加 7,004 億
元(+24.4%)。 
（三）達成率 
1.中央政府總預算業於 9 月 18 日公布，稅課收入較原預算案提高
6,000 億元，併計地方政府及特種基金後，全國稅課收入較預算案
提高 5,852 億元，本次發布均改依法定數計算預算達成率(實徵淨
額占全年預算數)。 
2.累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額占累計分配預算數 115.0%，占全年預算數
78.8%。 
圖 1 全國賦稅收入及年增率

說明：115 年 5-7 月年增率跳動較大，係因 114 年所得稅結算申報及繳納期限延長至 6 月底所致。

### Page 2

二、主要稅目別變動
（一）9 月實徵淨額與上年同月比較，以營業稅增加 297 億元、營利事業
所得稅增加 254 億元及證券交易稅增加 222 億元較多。
（二）累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額與上年同期比較，以證券交易稅增加 3,077
億元、營利事業所得稅增加 1,853 億元及營業稅增加 1,203 億元較多。
圖 2 主要稅目別變化
(115 年 1-9 月)
1.營利事業所得稅
(1)9 月實徵淨額 2,007 億元，較上年同月增加 254 億元(+14.5%)，主
因暫繳稅款成長。
(2)累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額 1 兆 28 億元，較上年同期增加 1,853 億元
(+22.7%)，主因結算申報自繳 、暫繳稅款 俱增及延分期稅款陸續
入帳。
2.綜合所得稅
9 月實徵淨額 490 億元，較上年同月增加 34 億元(+7.4%)，主因扣繳
稅款增加所致；累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額 8,279 億元，較上年同期增加
790 億元(+10.6%)，主因結算申報自繳增加，以及盈餘分配、薪資所

得等扣繳稅款均呈成長。
3.營業稅
9月實徵淨額1,414億元，較上年同月增加297億元(+26.6%)，累計1至
9月實徵淨額6,230億元，較上年同期增加1,203億元(+23.9%)，均因內
需穩定，加以電子零組件 、資通與視聽產品 等進口代徵稅額增加 ，
惟出口暢旺，適用外銷零稅率營業人申請退稅亦增，抵銷部分增幅。

### Page 3

3
4.貨物稅
(1)9月實徵淨額100億元，較上年同月減少11億元(-10.0%)，主因汽柴
油減徵幅度擴大，惟電動車大量到港推升進口 車稅額，抵銷部分
減幅。
(2)累計1至9月實徵淨額874億元，較上年同期減少207億元(-19.2%)，
除汽柴油降稅因素外 ，亦與新購汽機車及電動車之減免徵退稅增
加有關。
5.證券交易稅
(1)9 月及累計 1 至 9 月上市上櫃股票平均每日成交值為 1 兆 317 億
元、1 兆 1,461 億元，分別較上年同期(6,006 億元、4,451 億元)增
71.8%、1.6 倍。
(2)9 月 證 交 稅 實 徵 淨 額534 億 元， 較 上 年 同 月 增 加 222 億 元
(+71.2%)；累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額 5,060 億元，較上年同期增加
3,077 億元(+1.6 倍)。
6.關稅
9 月實徵淨額 157 億元，較上年同月增加 3 億元(+1.9%)，累計 1 至 9
月實徵淨額 1,224 億元，較上年同期增加 80 億元(+7.0%)，均因機械

及電機設備、基本金屬及其製品等進口稅額增加。
7.贈與稅
9 月實徵淨額 23 億元，較上年同月增加 6 億元(+35.7%)，累計 1 至 9
月實徵淨額 211 億元，較上年同期增加 43 億元(+25.3%)，主因大額
稅款案件增加。
8.土地增值稅
9 月實徵淨額 56 億元，較上年同月增加 12 億元(+26.6%)，主因大額
稅款案件增加；累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額 504 億元，較上年同期減少
14 億元(-2.8%)，主因房地交易量及大額稅款案件減少。
9.印花稅
9 月實徵淨額 31 億元，較上年同月增加 4 億元(+16.2%)，累計 1 至 9
月實徵淨額 175 億元，較上年同期增加 15 億元(+9.0%)，主因銀行放
款稅額成長。
（三）前 9月主要稅目實徵淨額占分配預算數之比率：
1.大於 100%者有證交稅(272%)、遺產稅(155%)、贈與稅(132%)、印花
稅(130%)、營業稅(116%)、關稅(114%)、契稅(111%)、房屋稅(106%)、

綜所稅(101%)、牌照稅(101%)及營所稅(101%)等。
2.低於 100%者有貨物稅(98%)、菸酒稅(97%)、土增稅(85%)、地價稅
(84%)及特銷稅(57%)等。

### Page 4

4
三、各級政府賦稅收入概況
(一)中央政府
累計 1 至 9 月中央政府賦稅收入 (不含中央統籌分配稅款 )實徵淨 額
2 兆 3,603 億元(占分配預算數 119.3%)，較上年同期增加 2,553 億元
(+12.1%)，雖新版財政收支劃分法 開始施行，營業稅劃歸中央政府 比
率大幅調降，減少 2,748 億元(-90.7%)，惟證交稅、所得稅分別增加 3,077
億元、2,230 億元，互抵後呈現上升。
(二)地方政府
累計 1 至 9 月地方政府賦稅收
入(含中央統籌分配稅款 )實徵
淨額 1 兆 898 億元(占分配預
算數 109.4%)，較上年同期增
加 4,527 億元(+71.0%)，主因
納入中央統籌分配 之營業稅
增加 4,030 億元(+2.1 倍)所致。
(三)中央特種基金
累計 1 至 9 月撥入中央特種基金之健康福利捐實徵淨額 195 億元，較
上年同期增加 2 億元(+0.8%)；房地合一課徵所得稅撥入住宅基金 135
億元，較上年同期減少 7 億元(-4.7%)；撥入長照基金 883 億元(其中 541

億元來自房地合一課徵所得稅、195 億元來自菸稅，146 億元來自遺贈
稅)，較上年同期增加 8 億元(+0.9%)。

価格指数の上昇は企業のコスト負担増を示唆

ユーロ圏総合PMIは節目の50超を維持。小売売上
高も小幅増加。ドイツの鉱工業生産は持ち直したが、
製造業受注は大型案件の反動で減少。景気は緩
やかな回復を維持するも、持続力には不透明感
欧
州
ASEAN経済は国ごとにばらつき。ベトナムは輸出や
鉱工業生産の好調を背景に高成⾧持続。フィリピン
では食料品やエネルギー価格高騰が消費の重石に。
タイでも原油高を背景にインフレ圧力が強まる
APAC
賃金改善が続く一方、個人消費の回復に弱さ。実
質消費支出は前年比減少が継続。消費者マインド

も小幅悪化し、物価高への警戒が根強い。景気動
向指数は低下したものの、基調判断は改善を維持
日
本
先週（10/5～10/12）発表の主な経済指標
米国では金融政策運営の変化と財政懸念、欧州では財政不
安が金利上昇圧力に。欧州では⾧期金利上昇による景気下
押し圧力が、ECBの追加利上げの必要性を低下させる可能性
金利・コスト上昇などの逆風下でも、設備投資意欲は旺盛。家
計の節約志向や企業間の格差は残るものの、価格転嫁・賃上
げも継続しており、日銀の想定するシナリオに沿った動き
注目点② 日銀さくらレポートは企業活動の底堅さを再確認注目点①米欧で金利上昇。異なる背景と金融政策への含意

（シェア）
米国：中間選挙を目前に住宅市場は軟調、アフォーダビリティへの不満が共和党への逆風に
 住宅ローン金利は足元で再び上昇
― 借入コストの負担増により、足元の住宅ローン申請件数は減少
 物価上昇が生活コストを押し上げるなか、金利上昇による住宅取得環境の悪化も家計の不満を助長する要因に
― トランプ大統領の生活費への政策対応は約7割が「期待より悪い」と評価しており、支持率は低下基調
―11月に控える中間選挙では、アフォーダビリティへの対応が引き続き主要な論点

 フランスでは国内政治・財政面の不安から長期金利が上昇。イタリア・ギリシャなど周辺の高債務国にも波及
― フランス国債の対独利回り差は急拡大し、一時欧州債務危機（2012年）以来の水準に。その後、大統領選の有力候補と

―ECBによる介入（国債買い入れ等）の可能性については、複数の高官が検討段階にないとの見方を示す

日本：金利・コスト上昇下でも企業活動は底堅く、設備投資意欲の強さを再確認
 10月のさくらレポートではAI関連需要の拡大とその裾野の広がりを背景に、企業活動の底堅さを確認
― 同一の企業コメントに登場するテーマの組み合わせを集計した共起分析では、「金利×設備投資」の組み合わせが増加。
金利上昇への意識が強まる一方、成長・省人化投資の旺盛なニーズは継続
 賃上げや資産効果が消費を下支えする一方、物価高を背景とした消費の二極化が継続
― 企業は価格転嫁を積極化するも、消費者の節約志向が根強く、商品競争力や顧客層によって転嫁余力に格差
⇒景気・物価は強弱入り混じるも、日銀の想定するシナリオに概ね沿った展開。12月利上げ見通しは不変

### Page 9

世界経済：エネルギー・食料の供給ショックによる負担は、所得階層や国によって格差
 生活費高騰に伴うインフレの影響は所得階層により格差
― 食料・エネルギー支出の割合が高い低所得層は負担が大。IMFは低所得層に絞った所得移転が望ましい対応と指摘
 エネルギー・食料の供給ショックによる影響は国家間でも格差
― 先進国では、エネルギー供給ショックによる消費の下押し効果は低所得層で特に大きい
― 食料品への支出割合が高い新興国・低所得途上国では、食料供給ショックによる消費の下押し効果が相対的に大きい
8

 タイでは過去にも大規模洪水が発生。2011年には、日系自動車企業の部材調達が滞り、日本国内生産に影響した経験も

― ただし、10月中旬にかけ更なる大雨が予想されており、操業停止の長期化や被害地域の拡大には依然警戒が必要

日本：個人消費は耐久財特需が剥落。今後は良好な所得環境を背景に緩やかに拡大
12
 8月の消費活動指数は、耐久財特需の剥落などにより、前年比＋0.9％と伸びが鈍化
― 耐久財消費は春先の自動車特需の反動から減少。食品などの非耐久財も、根強い節約志向を背景に弱含みが継続
― 底堅く推移してきたサービス消費も、台風・大雨や猛暑による外出控えの影響などから拡大ペースが鈍化
 今後は良好な所得環境が消費を下支え。個人消費は緩慢なペースながら、底堅く推移する見通し
― 実質所定内給与はプラス圏の伸びを維持しており、基調的な所得環境は良好。増加基調の購買力が消費を下支え
― ただし、インフレ率の再上昇で消費の回復ペースは緩慢。マインドの改善が一服したことも回復ペースを抑える要因に

（注） 旅行収支要因は、非居住者による国内消費（インバウンド消費）を控除し、居住者に
よる海外消費（アウトバウンド消費）を加えるための調整要因。アウトバウンド消費は
SNAの家計最終消費支出に含まれるが、GDPの計算上は輸入として控除される
（出所） 日本銀行「消費活動指数」より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成
消費活動指数（実質消費） 実質所定内給与の推移
（出所）厚生労働省「毎月勤労統計」、総務省「消費者物価指数」より、みずほ総合研究所
調査部作成

の伸びはプラス圏を維持

背景に弱含みが継続
耐久財特需の反動などから
実質消費の伸びが鈍化
自動車特需の
反動で減少
天候要因などで

日本：景気ウォッチャーは消費の緩やかな持ち直しを示唆も、家計・企業とも先行きには慎重
13
 家計動向（現状判断DI）は改善。9月の消費回復を示唆するも、回復ペースは緩慢
― 特にサービス関連の上昇が顕著。8月に増勢が一服したサービス消費は、大型連休や秋の行楽需要を受け持ち直し
― ただし、先行き判断DIは低下しており、個人消費全体でみれば緩やかな回復であることを示唆
 一方、企業動向は、現状・先行きとも慎重。景気回復の勢いは強くない
― 企業動向（現状判断DI）は製造業・非製造業ともに低下。AI関連など需要は堅調も、資材高などの供給制約が重石に

― 先行き判断についても、原材料高や金利上昇、中東情勢の不透明感などが重石となっている模様
（出所） 内閣府「景気ウォッチャー調査」より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成

## Document 029: 20261023【投研報告】市場快訊_AI資金壓力拖累科技類股，惟產業需求仍具韌性

Source PDF: all report pdf/20261023【投研報告】市場快訊_AI資金壓力拖累科技類股，惟產業需求仍具韌性.pdf
Report source: Yuanta (市場評論)
Date: 2026-10-23
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

確性不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行及本行之任何董事或受僱人，毋須負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決
策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元
大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
AI 資金壓力拖累科技股，惟產業需求仍具韌性
2026 年 10 月 23 日
一、 市場動態
1. 費半再度重挫，科技股成為美股主要賣壓來源：美國股市主要指數 10 月 22 日跌多漲
少， 道瓊工業指數上漲0.02%，收52,867.98 點；S&P 500 指數下跌 1.21%，收7,663.43
點；Nasdaq 綜合指數下跌 2.60%，收 26,008.86 點，創 6 月 5 日以來最大單日跌幅
。費城半導體指數重挫 6.72%，收 10,731.98 點，跌幅明顯大於主要股價指數，顯示
AI 及半導體族群仍是本波市場修正的主要壓力來源。

2. AI 相關企業全面承壓 ， 資金流向非科技股及公債 ：10 月 22 日 Meta 下跌約 7%、Lam
Research 跌幅逾一成，Nvidia 下跌 4.4%，其他 AI 相關股票亦明顯走低，顯示市場
對 AI 資本支出及投資報酬的疑慮已由大型科技平台延伸至半導體與基礎建設供應商
。不過，道瓊上漲 0.02%，羅素 2000 指數亦小幅收紅，反映資金並未全面撤出股市
；美國 10 年期公債殖利率則因避險需求跌破 5%，顯示資金由高評價科技股轉向其他
產業及避險資產。
3. 中東地緣政治風險升溫，油價攀至 5 月以來高點：中東能源運輸及供給風險 10 月 22
日再度升高，布蘭特原油價格上漲約 3.76%，升至每桶 113 美元附近，創 5 月以來高
點。油價攀升除使市場重新關注能源成本對通膨與 Fed 政策路徑的影響，也可能增加
AI 資料中心的電力及營運成本。中東情勢雖非本次半導體股重挫的唯一因素，但在市
場已擔憂 AI 投資效益的情況下，能源價格上揚進一步壓抑投資人風險偏好。

二、 下跌原因分析：
1. 殖利率回落難抵科技股下跌，顯示修正因素出現轉變：10 月 14 日費半重挫主要受到
美國 9 月 CPI 高於預期、升息疑慮及美債殖利率盤中急升影響，惟當日 10 年期殖利
率收盤反而小幅下降；10 月 22 日情況則更加明顯，美國 10 年期公債殖利率因避險
需求盤中跌破 5%，費半仍大跌 6.72%。這顯示市場關注焦點已由單純的利率與估值
壓力，逐步轉向 AI 資本支出的資金來源、融資能力及投資回收期。因此，即使後續殖
利率回落，若企業仍無法證明AI 投資能轉化為足夠營收與現金流，科技股評價仍可能
持續受到壓抑。
2. Meta 自由現金流轉負 ，AI 投資成本由預期轉為實際財務壓力 ：Meta 第三季營收 646.0
億美元、年增 26%，顯示核心業務仍維持強勁成長；但資本支出達 349.1 億美元，超
過同期營業現金流 327.9 億美元，使自由現金流由去年同期正 106.3 億美元轉為負
21.2 億美元。公司亦將全年資本支出預估調整至 1,350～1,450 億美元，並宣布設立

無表決權 Class C 股票，增加未來資本運用彈性。這反映 AI 投資規模已開始超越部分

### Page 2

確性不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行及本行之任何董事或受僱人，毋須負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決
策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元
大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
大型科技企業當期現金創造能力，但 Meta 仍持有 824.5 億美元現金、約當現金及有
價證券，不能將單季自由現金流轉負直接解讀為財務危機。
3. 資本稀缺逐漸成為 AI 擴張瓶頸，新興雲端業者面臨更大壓力：AI 發展需要持續投入
GPU、伺服器、資料中心及電力基礎建設，龐大資金需求使融資能力逐漸成為決定企
業成長速度的重要因素。相較具備穩定營業現金流與龐大資產規模的大型科技平台，
新興 AI 雲端服務商如 CoreWeave 及 Nebius 高度依賴外部融資，因而更容易受到借
貸成本、信用利差及投資人風險偏好變化影響。我們認為，這次修正並不代表 AI 運算

需求突然消失，而是市場開始重新評估不同企業能否負擔下一階段的擴張成本。
4. 半導體設備財報強勁卻遭賣壓，市場提高獲利兌現標準：Lam Research 於 10 月 21
日公布截至 2026 年 9 月季度（FY2027 Q1）營收 82.19 億美元，年增 54.4%、季增
22%，非 GAAP 營業利益率達 40.2%，下一季營收指引為 86 億美元、上下浮動 4 億
美元。然而，即使營運仍快速成長，10 月 22 日股價仍大幅下跌。我們研判，市場已
由過去重視 AI 訂單與營收成長，轉向同時檢視未來成長能否超越原先偏高預期，以及
AI 投資是否具備足夠的財務報酬。當股價已反映高度成長期待，即使財報表現良好，
也可能因評價調整與籌碼降溫而承壓。
5. 油價再度上漲，Fed 政策風險仍可能限制科技股評價：儘管 10 月 22 日美債殖利率因
避險需求下滑，但能源價格上漲仍可能延緩通膨降溫，使 Fed 後續政策路徑維持不確
定性。若油價持續高檔，不僅可能推升企業運輸、電力及營運成本，也將增加通膨預

期，進而限制長天期美債殖利率的下行空間。因此，科技股短線同時面臨AI 投資報酬
受到重新檢視 ， 以及能源價格與利率政策不確定的雙重壓力 。 後續10 月 28 日 FOMC
會後聲明及主席記者會，將是評估利率環境的重要依據。
三、 市場看法：
1. 股市下跌但多數個股上漲 ， 顯示賣壓集中於大型科技股 ：10 月 22 日 S&P 500 指數下
跌 1.21%，但根據美聯社（AP）報導，約三分之二成分股逆勢上漲，追蹤 S&P 500
等權重指數的 ETF（RSP）更上漲 0.73%，道瓊工業指數亦小幅收紅，呈現市值加權
指數與市場廣度明顯背離的現象 。這反映本波修正主要集中於權重較高的 AI 及大型科
技股，而非整體美股同步走弱。相較上週以防禦性類股輪動為主的情況，本週更值得
注意的是市場對高資本支出及高評價科技企業的重新定價，資金仍持續流向其他產業
，而非全面性風險撤離。只要賣壓未進一步擴散，現階段仍較接近科技股評價調整與
類股輪動，而非整體美股多頭格局反轉。
2. 台積電財測再度上修，顯示 AI 需求尚未出現結構性反轉：台積電 10 月 15 日法說會

將 2026 年美元營收成長預估由原先略高於 40%上修至約 44%， 第三季營收達新台幣
1.49 兆元、年增 50.9%，稅後純益年增 68.7%，每股盈餘 29.43 元、同樣年增 68.7%
；第四季美元營收預估為 523～543 億美元，以中間值計算季增約 14%。此外，Lam
Research 截至 9 月季度營收年增 54.4%，亦顯示半導體設備需求仍強勁。前述這些

### Page 3

確性不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行及本行之任何董事或受僱人，毋須負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決
策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元
大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
數據與近期科技股重挫形成明顯對比 ， 反映目前市場主要擔憂AI 投資回收及股票評價
，而非半導體終端需求同步惡化。只要大型雲端服務商未全面縮減AI 資本支出，先進
製程與設備需求中期仍具支撐。
3. AI 投資進入報酬驗證階段，營收成長能否追上資本投入成為關鍵：我們認為，AI 產業
目前正由過去的資本支出擴張階段，逐步轉向投資報酬率與自由現金流的驗證階段。
以 Meta 為例，第三季營收仍成長 26%，但資本支出已超過營業現金流，顯示即使
AI 技術有助於改善廣告推薦及營收變現，龐大基礎建設投入仍可能使短期自由現金流

承壓。不過，資料中心及運算設備通常具有多年使用年限，不能僅以單季現金流評估
整體投資成敗。真正關鍵在於未來 AI 服務收入、營業利益及現金流成長，能否逐步抵
銷前期資本投入。具備明確商業化模式、較高資本效率及穩定現金流的企業，後續表
現可望優於高度依賴融資與成長預期的業者。
4. AI 融資風險仍集中於部分企業 ， 尚不足以認定信用危機全面擴散 ：Meta Q3 財報顯示
，該公司持有 824.5 億美元現金、約當現金及有價證券，顯示其具備一定財務緩衝能
力；相較之下，新興AI 雲端業者因資料中心建置成本龐大、現金流尚未成熟，對信用
市場與外部融資條件的敏感度相對較高 。雖然 AI 資本支出引發投資人重新評估企業信
用風險，但我們認為目前仍缺乏足夠證據認定風險已擴散至整體投資級債市場或銀行
體系。然而，後續仍宜密切觀察 AI 相關企業 CDS 與公司債利差變化、再融資能力及
主要金融機構授信條件對於科技類股及整體股債市場的後續影響。
5. 股價修正不代表企業獲利同步下修，財報表現仍有機會支撐科技股：儘管近期半導體

及 AI 相關股價回落，但台積電與Lam Research 最新財報顯示，部分供應鏈企業營收
及獲利仍維持高度成長，反映股票價格與企業基本面短期可能出現明顯落差。當市場
因融資及資本支出疑慮而降低評價倍數時，即使企業獲利持續增加，股價也可能暫時
承壓。然而，若後續大型科技公司公布的雲端營收、AI 訂單與獲利展望持續優於預期
，企業 EPS 上修仍有機會抵銷部分本益比壓縮壓力。因此，目前尚不宜將科技股價格
修正直接等同於 AI 產業景氣反轉，企業獲利能否持續兌現，仍是中期市場方向的重要
支撐。

### Page 4

確性不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行及本行之任何董事或受僱人，毋須負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決
策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元
大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
6. Fed 與大型科技股財報進入驗證期，短線震盪可能延續：Fed 將於 10 月 27～28 日舉
行利率決策會議，市場除關注是否調整政策利率外，更應重視會後聲明及主席記者會
對通膨、油價與後續利率路徑的態度。另一方面，大型雲端服務商後續公布的財報，
將提供 AI 資本支出、雲端營收及投資效率的最新證據。短線科技股仍可能受到市場評
價偏高、融資風險及地緣政治因素影響而維持震盪；不過，只要企業獲利成長與 AI
需求未出現結構性反轉，中期科技產業基本面仍具支撐，現階段維持短線審慎、中期
偏正向的看法。
四、市場展望：

1. 科技股短線宜審慎觀察 ， 中期仍看好具基本面支撐的AI 產業 ：近期科技股受到 AI 資本支
出、融資能力與評價調整等因素影響，短線波動可能延續，尤其高度依賴外部資金及獲利
尚未成熟的 AI 基礎建設業者，仍面臨較大不確定性。然而，台積電及 Lam Research 最
新財報顯示，先進製程與半導體設備需求尚未出現全面轉弱。我們認為，科技股中期展望
仍偏正向，但市場將更重視企業營收成長、訂單能見度、自由現金流及資本效率。相較缺
乏穩定獲利的 AI 概念企業，具技術優勢、財務結構穩健及獲利成長能見度的科技企業，
風險報酬相對有利。
2. AI 產業由全面成長轉向基本面分化，現金流將成為選股關鍵：隨著大型科技企業持續擴
大 AI 基礎建設投資，市場對未來投資報酬率的要求將同步提高。就目前已公布的科技股
財報結果來看，AI 產業長期成長趨勢尚未改變，但後續股價表現將更加分化，具備穩定
現金流、明確訂單及商業化能力的企業，相對有利；反之，對於高度依賴外部融資、自由
現金流持續為負且缺乏明確獲利時程的企業，仍宜維持審慎。

3. 美債避險功能重現，中天期債券風險報酬相對均衡：半導體類股 10/22 重挫，美國公債
價格上揚、10 年期殖利率盤中跌破 5%，顯示股市風險偏好下降時，美債仍可提供一定
避險效果。雖然 Fed 政策路徑及能源價格仍存在不確定性，但預期相較受政策利率影響
較大的短天期公債，以及受財政赤字、國債供給與期限溢價影響的超長天期公債，中天期
美國公債的風險報酬仍相對較為均衡 。 投資級債因企業信用基本面整體仍具韌性 ， 中期維
持偏正向看法；高收益債則須留意 AI 相關發行人信用風險上升及利差擴大的影響。

雙鴻為散熱解決方案供應商 
 雙鴻為散熱解決方案供應商 
雙鴻成立於 1998 年 8 月 ， 為國內前二大NB 散熱模組廠 ， 過去主要生產散熱器 、
散熱片、散熱模組、散熱導管、風扇等，現在已轉型為提供客戶完整散熱解決
方案供應商。主要應用於筆記型電腦、桌上型電腦、智慧型手機及伺服器等領
域。2Q26 產品應用領域 Server 78%、PC 17%、VGA/Gaming 4%、及Others(手
機及車用) 1%。另外水冷業務比重於 1H26 約為 55~60%。

營運逐季成長，氣冷及水冷兩塊
業務均樂觀

營運逐季成長，氣冷及水冷兩塊業務均樂觀 
2025 年雙鴻水冷營收比重 35%，1Q26 提升至 55%，帶動公司業績大幅增長。
氣冷部分，2024 年月營收 2-2.5 億元，2025 年 4～4.5 億元，2026 年平均 6～
7 億元，氣冷水冷均樂觀，以 AWS TR3 來看，氣冷及水冷比重至少是 70~80%
對比 20~30%，TR4 將有機會為 100%全水冷 ， 水冷大趨勢不變 ，VR 9 月起量，
TR3 9 月起量 Compute tray 及 Switch tray 及 Rack manifolds 都有相關產品出
貨，Compute tray 水冷產值 3000usd 以上，Switch tray 水冷產值 1000usd 以
上，ASIC 水冷產值高於 GPU 客戶，2H26~2027 年 ASIC 增長可期。

1H26 GB 水冷營收中 Rack manifolds 佔比 30-35%、Cold Plate+inner manifolds
佔比 55-60%、CDU 5% ，2026 年可視為 CDU 元年，2027 年營收跳增可期。
公司展望 3Q26 營收+15~20%QOQ，實際+12.5%QOQ 低於預期，4Q26 營收
QOQ+雙位數，2026 年營收+70~85%YOY，3Q26 毛利率將回到 29.7%以上水
準，2027 年維持正向看法。

維持買進，目標價 1800 元

維持買進評等，目標價 1800 元 
2Q26 毛利率低於預期為一次性因素，氣冷及水冷兩塊業務樂觀，水冷擴張趨勢
明確，故維持買進，預估2026 年 EPS 55.74 元，預估 2027 年 EPS 90.36 元，
目標價 1800 元。(2027 PER 20x)

風險提示 
出貨不如預期，ASP 不如預期。

時碩 (4566  TT)  Trading Buy

2H26 營運明顯增長 ，投資建議 Trading Buy 。  
投 資 建 議 Trading Buy ： 雖 目 前 時碩 (4566 TT) 處於 CB 緘 默 期 ，
未 提 供 明 確 展 望 ， 但 受 惠 於 航 太 、 散 熱 產 品 出 貨 增 長 ，3Q26 營
運 已 明 顯 攀 升 。 考 量 2026 、 2027 年 營 運 動 能 向 上 ， 投 資 建 議
Trading Buy ， 目 標 價 92 元 (以 202 7 年 EPS 及 PE  17 倍 評 價 )。  
時 碩 積 極 布 局 高 成 長 潛 力 的 利 基 型 市 場 ， 包 含 液 冷 散 熱 系 統 、
人 形 機 器 人 零 組 件 ： 液冷散熱 方面， 時 碩 致 力 於 提 供 一 站 式 的
全 方 位 熱 管 理 解 決 方 案 ， 其 產 品 線 涵 蓋 快 接 頭 、 分 歧 管 、 冷 卻
液 分 配 單 元 、 微 通 道 板 及 技 術 門 檻 較 高 的 水 冷 板 ， 並 能 提 供 不
鏽 鋼 與 銅 等 多 樣 材 質 。公司 已 成 功 獲 得 美 、 日 系 液 冷 系 統 大 廠
訂單，且於 2H26 放量 出貨 ， 預估 散 熱 產 品 佔 202 6 年 營 收 比 重
有望 接近 10% 。 人 形 機 器 人方 面 ， 時 碩 積 極 拓 展 此 領 域 的 關 鍵
零 組 件 業 務 ， 產 品 涵 蓋 諧 波 減 速 機 關 鍵 零 組 件 、 機 器 手 臂 關 節
精 密 機 械 加 工 零 件 。 時 碩 目 前 已 打 入 美 國 與 中 國 廠 商 的 供 應 鏈
體 系 ； 中 國 無 錫 廠 藉 由 地 緣 優 勢 積 極 切 入 關 節 零 件 製 造 。 泰 國
春 武 里 新 廠也 規 劃 將 產 線 逐 步 延 伸 至 機 器 人 相 關 應 用 領 域 。 
3Q26 營 收 明 顯 躍 升，預估 2026 、2027 年 營 運 動 能 向 上：雖 1H26
獲 利 表 現 平 平 ， 但 受 惠 於 航 太 、 散 熱 應 用 出 貨 明 顯 增 長 帶 動 ，
3Q26 營收 18.18 億元， QoQ+23.8 6%、 YoY+43.23% ； 且 出 貨 動
能 有 望 延 伸 至 4Q26 。2026 年 方 面 ，汽 車 、 航 太 、 工 業 三 大 領 域
皆有望較 2025 年 增 長。汽 車 應 用 方 面， 雖 中 國 市 場 競 爭 仍 然 激
烈，但 因 切 入 新 能 源 車 領 域 且 客 戶 需 求 帶 動 下，營 收穩 定 增 長。
航太領域 方面， 天 陽 航 太 已 通 過 多 項 航 太 認 證 ， 成 功 打 入 全 球
前 兩 大 飛 機 製 造 商 及 Tier 1 供 應 商 ， 預 估 營 收 將維 持 高 速 成 長
態勢 。 工 業 應 用 方 面 ， 既 有 領 域 有 望 較2025 年 改 善 ， 且 新 成 長
動 能 將 來 自 於 液 冷 散 熱 相 關 產 品 及 人 形 機 器 人 零 組 件 。 預 估
2026 年 營 收 66.00 億 元 ，YoY+ 32.62 %， 毛 利 率 維 持 高 檔 ， 營 業
利益 YoY+ 97.15 %。 預 估 稅 後 純 益 3.71 億 元 ， YoY+ 148.83 %，
EPS 3.36 元。 隨 著 航 太 、 散 熱 應 用 持 續 發 酵 ， 且 泰 國 新 廠 建 置
完 成 ， 預 估 2027 年 營 收 、 獲 利 動 能 向 上 。

2 群益投顧 
  時碩 (4566  TT) E SG ：  
時碩的 ESG 政 策 由 「 永 續 發 展 暨 風 險 管 理 委 員 會 」 統 籌 ， 並 由 「 風 險 管 理
推 動 委 員 會 」 負 責 執 行 與 追 蹤，將 氣 候 變 遷 及 各 項 永 續 議 題 納 入 公 司 整 體 策
略 與 風 險 管 理 流 程 中。在 環 境 面，公 司 依 循 國 際 標 準 進 行 溫 室 氣 體 盤 查 與 第
三 方 確 信 ， 並 透 過 節 能 設 備 導 入 、 能 源 管 理 與 監 測 系 統 、 製 程 優 化 、 太 陽 能
及綠電等多元措施推動減碳與能源效率提升，同時強化水資源與廢棄物管
理 ， 藉 由 回 收 再 利 用 、 廢 水 循 環 及 資 源 化 設 備 ， 落 實 減 量 及 循 環 經 濟 ； 原 物
料 與 包 材 則 遵 循 RoHS 、REACH 等 規 範，降 低 製 程 對 環 境 的 衝 擊。社 會 面 方
面，時 碩 重 視 員 工 薪 資 福 利、培 訓 與 職 涯 發 展，推 動 性 別 平 等 與 管 理 階 層 多
元 化，並 建 立 職 業 安 全 衛 生 制 度 及 教 育 訓 練，以 降 低 職 災 風 險；在 供 應 鏈 則
透 過 定 期 稽 核 與 評 分 機 制，提 升 供 應 商 在 品 質、交 期 與 服 務 上 的 表 現，並 以
產 品 品 質 與 安 全 為 核 心 ， 確 保 生 產 與 產 品 符 合 相 關 要 求 。 同 時 ， 公 司 以「 企
業 公 民 」 自 我 定 位，投 入 社 會 公 益 與 在 地 關 懷，減 少 營 運 對 社 區 的 負 面 影 響，
並 維 持 完 善 的 資 訊 安 全 管 理 以 避 免 資 訊 外 洩。在 治 理 面，時 碩 建 構 包 含 獨 立
董 事 與 女 性 董 事 的 董 事 會 結 構，設 置 薪 酬 與 審 計 等 功 能 性 委 員 會，訂 定 涵 蓋
營 運 、 財 務、 法 遵 、 環 境 與 氣 候 變 遷 、 人 資 與 人 權 等 面 向 的 風 險 管 理 政 策 ，
並 透 過 法 說 會、網 站 與 永 續 報 告 與 利 害 關 係 人溝 通，同 時 積 極 參 與 各 項 ESG
獎 項 評 選 ， 以 強 化 公 司 治 理 與 永 續 形 象 。

### Page 6

個股報告

6 群益投顧 
  【 投 資 評 等 說 明 】 
  評等  定義  
  強力買進 (Strong Buy)  首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間≥ 35%  
  買進 (Buy)  15%≤ 首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間<35%  
  區間操作 (Trading Buy)  5%≤ 首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間<15 %

中立 (Neutral)  
無 法 由 基 本 面 給 予 投 資 評 等 
預 期 近 期 股 價 將 處 於 盤 整 
建 議 降 低 持 股

【 投 資 評 等 調 整 機 制 】 
  投 資 評 等 係「 首 次 」給 予 特 定 個 股 投 資 評 等 時，其 前 一 日 收 盤 價 相 較3 個月
目 標 價 之 潛 在 上 漲 空 間 計 算 而 得 。 個 股 投 資 評 等 分 為四 個 等 級 ， 定 義 如 上 。
爾 後 的 投 資 評 等係 依 循 「 首 次 評 等 」 ， 直 到 停 止 推 薦 。  
「 強 力 買 進 」、「 買 進 」及 「 區 間 操 作 」均有 upside 目 標 價 。差 別 僅 在 於 ，
首 次 評 等 時 潛 在 報 酬 率 不 同 。 「 中 立 」 則 無 目 標 價 。 
一 旦 我 們 給 予 特 定 個 股「 強 力 買 進 」、「 買 進 」或「 區 間 操 作 」之 投 資 評 等，
就 是 責 任 的 開 始，爾 後 將 透 過 各 式 研 究 報 告 作 定 期 性、持 續 性 追 蹤 基 本 面 及
股 價 變 化 ， 直 到 停 止 推 薦 。 
停 止 推 薦 情 境 ： 
1.  達 目 標 價 。 
2.  雖 未 達 原 訂 目 標 價 ， 但 檢 視 基 本 面 、 訊 息 面 、 籌 碼 面 等 多 方 訊 息 ， 研
判 股 價 上 漲 空 間 已 然 有 限 ， 將 適 時 出 具 降 評 報 告 。 
3.  推 薦 後 股 價 不 漲 反 跌 ， 亦 將 出 具 降 評 報 告 。

【免責聲明】  
  本 研 究 報 告 僅 提 供 予 特 定 人 之 客 戶 作 為 參 考 資 料「 非 經 同 意 不 得 轉 載 」 。我
們 並 不 確 保 此 資 訊 的 完 整 性 與 正 確 性，投 資 人 應 了 解，報 告 中 有 關 未 來 預 測
之 陳 述 可 能 不 會 實 現，因 而 不 應 被 依 賴。而 且 此 報 告 並 非 根 據 特 定 投 資 目 的
或 依 預 定 對 象 之 財 務 狀 況 所 撰 寫 出 來 的，因 此，此 研 究 報 告 的 目 的，既 非 對
投 資 人 於 買 賣 證 券、選 擇 權、期 貨 或 其 他 證 券 相 關 之 衍 生 性 商 品 提 供 詢 價 服
務，亦 非 作 為 進 行 交 易 的 要 約。投 資 人 應 注 意 到 相 關 證 券 之 價 值 及 收 益，可
能會有無預警地上升或下降，產生投資回報金額可能比原始投資來得少的
情形。

晶呈科技(4768.TT) 
產業：化學工業 
氣體原料量產降本效應與玻璃加工產品放量，推動明年獲利高成⻑ 
 投資評價與建議 
考量三廠C4F6⼀級合成放量顯著優化⽑利率重回30%，且AI驅動TGV及CPO
加工產品步入規模化量產，⻑期動能強勁，參考台股半導體特化與先進封裝概
念股本益比30-35倍，因短期受高額研發費用投入、新廠折舊與轉虧為盈初期
致使當前本益比失真，故採用TGV產線滿載Forward PER。 
 重點評論分析 
特氣出貨回升，TGV量產放量：推估4Q26隨特氣出貨回升及客⼾去庫存告⼀
段落，營收可望季增73.7%至6.6億元，全年營收估增50.9%至18.6億元，玻璃
加工受惠AI高階封裝與CPO需求，GCB及One Piece Glass FAU已進入規模量
產，預估2H26營收較上半年成⻑230%，惟三廠延至4Q26試量產，特氣原料
成本改善遞延，⽑利率下修至23%，加上研發費用增加，使稅後EPS 0.29元。  
⼀級合成與高⽑利產品帶動獲利躍升： 預估2027年營收年增35.1%至25.1億
元，受惠AI ASIC大廠特氣需求放量、TGV及矽光子共同封裝相關應用擴產6倍
產能，並拓展生醫檢測市場，預計3Q27末相關產線陸續量產，Stripper隨認證
客⼾增加至9家，⽑利率因三廠C4F6⼀級合成與純化工廠預計1Q27放量，年
產能250噸，可降低外購原料成本，加上Stripper產能利用率提升，以及TGV、
生醫檢測及Stripper等高⽑利產品營收占比提升至20%，帶動年增7.92ppts至
30.9% ， 營益率升至12.5% ， 稅前盈餘年增936%至2.91億元 ， 稅後EPS 4.30元 。  
 投資風險 
⼀級合成新廠量產進度與高⽑利產品TGV 、 特化投產或客⼾導入進度不如預期 。

### Page 2

2 
| 公司簡介  
公司所做的玻璃通孔技術(Through Glass Via ,TGV)主要應用於大積體電路玻璃載板(Glass Core Su bstrate, 
GCS)(非 Glass interposer)，是做為⼀個玻璃核心晶圓加工廠(TGV Glass Foundry)，並將加工好 的玻璃核心層
(Glass Core Board, GCB)，交付給下游 IC 載板廠或封測廠後，在玻璃核心的外側壓合 ABF 絕緣膜，並製作精細
的銅線路 ， 組合成完整的 GCS ． 亦透過玻璃加工技術開發⼀體成型式 CPO 光纖束導基座(One Piece Glass FAU)。 
GCB 是數個晶片叢共用的超大玻璃地基，支撐所有晶片和並保持封裝的平坦和穩定，解決穩定性(平坦度與結構支
撐)的瓶頸，位置在整個封裝體的最底層，玻璃材料解決熱膨脹、曲蹺度、以及對封裝尺寸的要求極限，目前公司
的製作玻璃微孔的貫穿深度與孔道寬度(孔徑)的比值已達到深寬比 100:1，全孔立體型態增圓度可達 99%、板厚
可達 1.85mm，單位面積產值相較 Glass Interposer 多 25-30 倍，在硬脆且易碎的玻璃材質上實現百倍深寬比，
是公司透過獨家氣態分解融蝕技術，在微觀垂直孔道上的極致控制力。 
One Piece Glass FAU 是⼀種應用於 CPO(共同封裝光學)與超高速光通訊系統中光電整合模組，超微細光纖束的
精準對位組裝，專為插裝與固定直徑僅 125 微米(µm)的超微細光纖束所設計(直徑極細，大約三根光纖才等於⼀
根頭髮的粗細)，在 AI 晶片架構從傳統電訊號傳輸轉向光訊號傳輸的過程中，該產品主要解決(1)微米級光纖對位
極難與光訊號損耗問題，公司雷射改質與氣態蝕刻技術(LADY)，在玻璃上打造出高⼀致性的微孔結構，提供高精
度的光纖對位，顯著提高光耦合效率(Optical Coupling Efficiency)，可減少多件式組裝產生的累積公差(2)高溫運
轉下的熱膨脹與光軸偏斜，利用玻璃材料極低熱膨脹係數的物理特性，確保光纖對位結構在⻑期高溫運算下仍保
持極佳的尺寸，(3)光軸穩定性以及光電整合元件的結構精密性，採用⼀體成型(One-Piece)的玻璃基座設計，結合
TGV 製程的高垂直度與高精度特性，配合客⼾在 AI 系統架構升級中對光電整合元件的規模化部署需求。 
圖表、Glass Core Board (左圖)不同象限與One Piece Glass FAU(右圖)顯微鏡下示意圖

資料來源：玉山投顧、公司資料 
| 營  運  概  況  
外銷與特氣出貨拉動累計營業收入倍增，受費用提升致⽑利率承壓：1H26 合併營收 8.21 億元，YoY+141%，主
要受惠特殊氣體外銷出口的大幅增加，氣體出貨支數達 13,022 支，其中 2Q26 營收 QoQ-13.9%至 3.8 億元，主
因在於部分外銷出口出貨量有所減少至 6,294 支，受韓國等記憶體客⼾要求調整出貨影響，但⽑利率
QoQ+11.1ppts 至 24.4%，因低⽑利產品(海外記憶體客⼾的特氣)相對 1Q26 出貨較少，1H26 ⽑利率 YoY-
20.45ppts 至 18.5%，除了產品組合關係，另因上游⼀級原料合成⾃給率尚未開出，使得原料成本負擔較沉重，
2Q26 費用率從 17.6%提升至 25.6%，主要來⾃研究發展費用大幅提升從 7.1%提升到 12.2%，目的在於為竹北五
廠、頭份三廠等新廠建置進行關鍵技術研發，1H26 稅前虧損 0.36 億元，稅後 EPS -0.99 元。 
特氣與 TGV 放量帶動 2H26 營收優於 1H26：3Q26 單季營收 3.8 億元，ＱoQ-0.6%，推估 3Q26 鋼瓶出貨量
6,000-6,200 支，主要來⾃ C4F6 內外銷出貨持續，⽑利率維持 24%，推估 4Q26 營收 QoQ+73.7%至 6.6 億元， 
4Q26 鋼瓶出貨量 7,500-7,800 支，2H26 鋼瓶出貨合計約 13,500-14,000 支，比 1H26 出貨量成⻑約 7.7%，隨
著全球半導體大廠的新產能與新廠陸續開出，帶動對氣體 C4F6 蝕刻氣體、Laser Gas 等的剛性需求持續增加，另

### Page 3

3 
外 2H26 隨客⼾去庫存化使需求回補，帶動氣體鋼瓶出貨量提升，並受惠於 AI 伺服器與高階封裝和矽光子共同封
裝(Co-Packaged Optics, CPO)，使玻璃加工市場快速成⻑，帶動相關特殊氣體的需求同步提升。 
玻璃加工技術在 TGV Glass Foundry 領域應用，已成功建置兩大可量產的核心產品線，配合年底 2 條產線(月產
能約 1 萬片)到位與量產放量，預估 2H26 營收相較 1H26 成⻑ 230%，主因為 GCB 與 One Piece Glass FAU 皆
已跨越工程驗證，正式進入單月接單與出貨片數達四位數以上的規模量產的放量階段，預估單片 150x150mm(5
萬孔/片)價值約$500-700 美元。 
特化產品 Stripper 目前設有⼀條年產能 288 頓產線，目前已被客⼾認證達 15 個品項，其中 7 個已達穩定交貨，
驗證通過的客⼾數已增加至９家，預估 2H26 營收相較 1H26 成⻑ 3%，加計其他產品，預估 2026 全年營收成⻑
率下修至 50.9%達 18.5 億元 ， 由於三廠(C4F6 ⼀級合成工廠)投產時程 ， 從先前 3Q26 延後至 4Q26 進行試量產 ，
使全年⽑利率從原先預估的 35%下修至 23%，加上研發費用擴張，下修稅前盈餘年成⻑ 41.3%至 0.28 億元，稅
後E P S  0 . 2 9元 。  
⼀級合成放量大幅優化特氣成本結構，TGV 導入 AI 大廠放量，帶動獲利大幅成⻑：邁入 2027 年，預估全年營收
成⻑ 35.1%至 25.1 億元，主要成⻑來⾃特殊氣體核心業務持續受惠 AI ASIC 大廠需求放量，另外玻璃加工應用於
AI 先進封裝與矽光子共同封裝產品預計於 3Q27 末累計建置達 12 條產線並正式量產，推動營收規模呈指數級成
⻑，在生醫檢測應用(竹北五廠)的最新進程上，已於生醫園區簽約購入竹北五廠房，將玻璃加工技術應用於生產生
醫用檢測儀器，在玻璃培養⽫微雕出高⼀致性的 3D 微孔陣列，模擬人體內真實的立體腫瘤微環境(3D 全立體型
態載具(Booster))，針對 EGFR 基因突變陽性的局部晚期非小細胞肺癌細胞進行精準控制併用標靶藥物泰格沙
(Tagrisso®)之細胞檢測實驗體外評估(In Vitro) ， 提升藥物對癌細胞的抑制與殺傷成效 ， 並藉由開發的生醫檢測儀
器，作為癌症精準用藥的關鍵數據，預計五廠將於 3Q27 末正式開始量產，此應用將使玻璃加工技術跨足高⽑利
生醫市場，現有廠房空間至少可規劃 6 條產線，後續將視客⼾需求進行滾動擴充。Stripper 隨 2026 年認證客⼾
增加朝向產能滿載，以及二廠無水氟化氫(AHF)量產線與三氯化硼(BCl3)試產線裝機完成，四條產線年產值上看 3-
4 億元的效益下，穩定貢獻營收。2027 全年⽑利率則受惠頭份三廠的 C4F6 ⼀級合成與純化工廠預計於 1Q27 正
式放量，設計年產能 250 噸，特氣⾃給率將大幅拉升，改變過去依賴外購二、三級純化原料的成本結構，使成本
大幅降低，加上特用化學品業務在產能利用率拉升，高⽑利產品如 TGV 玻璃載板、生醫檢測儀器、Stripper ⾃製
放量的佔比合計提升至 20%，使⽑利率顯著提升 7.92ppts 至 30.9%，費用率隨營業收入規模擴大而稀釋至 18.36%，
營益率提升 9.75ppt 至 12.5%，稅前盈餘年成⻑ 936%至 2.91 億元，稅後 EPS 4.30 元。

2 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
The Five Shocks of 2026—And the Extraordinary Resilience of the U.S. Economy
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
This is the year the consensus got mugged.
Rewind to the start of the year. The market consensus was that the U.S. economy was
set to slow. The Fed was set to cut interest rates. Corporate earnings were set to grow—

but not explode. Energy prices would remain tame. And the odds looked good that the
Republicans would control the House, the Senate, or both, following the midterm election.
Nine months on, the landscape looks starkly different. Defined by five shocks—energy,
interest rates, earnings, growth, and politics—2026 looks nothing like the year investors
thought they were buying on January 1.
Shock 1: Diesel Matters. While global oil prices have moved higher since the start of

the U.S./Iran war on February 28, oil prices have yet to surpass their previous record high
because the world has released some 400 million barrels from strategic reserves this year,
China has curbed its demand for oil imports, and the U.S. has goosed its energy exports to
record highs.
But here is the problem: While gasoline powers the American consumer, diesel powers the
American economy—and diesel prices are now sitting at record highs. Diesel supplies have

been hammered by wars in the Middle East and Ukraine that have sharply reduced refining
capacity. According to the International Energy Agency, by August, exports of diesel from
refiners in the Middle East had fallen by a quarter from prewar levels. Meanwhile, Ukraine
has hit 24 of Russia’s 34 largest refineries, reducing supplies from one of the world’s
largest diesel exporters. The upshot: U.S. diesel prices have soared from $3.55 a gallon
at the start of the year to roughly $6.52 a gallon. While crude oil trades for around $100

a barrel, the retail equivalent of a barrel of diesel now costs more than $260. The diesel
shock of 2026 was the last thing investors saw coming—and remains a key impediment to
structurally lower inflation.
Shock 2: From Interest Rate Cuts to Rate Hikes. Perhaps no other consensus view
entering 2026 has been turned more dramatically on its head than expectations for the
Fed. Heading into 2026, markets were pricing in rate cuts on expectations of a cooling

labor market, moderating inflation, and slower economic growth. The Fed easing cycle was
expected to continue in 2026. In January 2026, futures showed a roughly 50% chance of a
cut by March.
Expectations, however, flipped in late February with the start of the U.S./Iran war. The
Middle East energy shock reignited inflationary pressures, which, combined with stronger-
than-expected U.S. economic growth and a tightening labor market, have demolished the

odds of easier money in 2026. Short- and long-term Treasury yields rose sharply last week
in the wake of the S&P Global Flash U.S. Composite Purchasing Managers’ Index (PMI)
which pointed to the strongest business expansion in more than five years and the fastest
employment growth in over four years, while also reinforcing expectations that the Fed
will maintain a restrictive policy stance for longer. Markets were expecting two to three
quarter-point rate cuts in 2026; instead, the Fed has already raised rates once this year and

is expected to keep raising rates over the near term.
Shock 3: The Earnings Boom No One Saw Coming. Not all the shocks of 2026 are
negative. Indeed, stronger-than-expected corporate earnings have been among the most
positive factors of this year. Corporate America entered the year facing questions about
tariffs, AI-related capital expenditure spending, the health of the U.S. consumer, operating
leverage and the U.S. dollar outlook. At the start of the year, Wall Street was expecting

roughly $290 of S&P 500 earnings per share for 2026, per Bloomberg estimates. But by
September 21, the estimate had risen by $50, to $340 per share.
Investment Implications
While some of these shocks may
contribute to periods of market
volatility, the combination of strong
earnings growth, robust domestic
demand, and ongoing capital
investment suggests that the
fundamental backdrop for Equities
remains constructive.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Earnings have been upgraded and underpinned by several factors, including robust final 
domestic demand in the U.S., a pickup in global growth, AI-related capital spending, 
productivity gains, and enhanced operating leverage. All these variables have helped deliver 
an earnings “shock” to Wall Street. And as an aside, yes, headline profit growth has been 
inflated by strong technology earnings and one-off investment gains from Amazon and 
Alphabet. That said, the S&P 500’s earnings story remains extraordinary. According to 
FactSet, the 493 companies excluding the Magnificent 7 1 
posted 31.8% earnings growth in 
Q2, the strongest level since late 2021.

Shock 4: An Economy with more Horsepower than the Headlines Suggest. That 
corporate profits have exceeded expectations this ye
ar is due in no small part to an economy 
that continues to surprise to the upside. The Fed was supposed to cut rates this year to 
keep the economy humming and prevent it from losing steam. But rather than limp through 
2026, the economy has leaped ahead. Yes, real gross domestic product (GDP) growth of 
2.1% in Q1 and 1.5% in Q2 are hardly blockbuster annualized rates of growth. However, the 
figures belie the underlying strength of the U.S. economy, with real final domestic demand

(which strips out trade) running at a 4.2% annual rate in Q2. According to the latest reading 
of the Atlanta Fed GDP tracker, Q3 real GDP grow
th is running at a red-hot rate of 5.1%. 
Slowdown? What slowdown?
Resilient consumer spending, robust capital investment, record leve
ls of new business 
formation, and strong foreign direct investment inflows have combined to stoke growth 
in the $32 trillion behemoth called the U.S. economy—the most resilient and dynamic 
economy in the world.
Shock 5: The Unexpected Washington Reset. The last shock is still in the making, but 
given current trends, the odds are rising that the Democrats could control both the House 
and Senate follow
ing the midterm elections. To this point, in January, and per Kalshi, the 
Democrats had a 71% shot at the House and an 18% chance of taking the Senate. Fast-
forward to today, and the odds are a 90% chance that the Democrats take the House and a 
60% chance that they take the Senate.
How all of this plays out remains to be seen—there are some 36 days before the November 
vote, and anything can happen. The market base case remains that the Democrats take the 
House, and the Senate is a toss-up—or too close to call. But with the Democrats expected to 
control at least one chamber of Congress, inv
estors can expect more volatility and uncertainty 
about negotiations ov
er the budget, debt ceiling, defense spending and AI regulation. Another 
political wildcard to watch: the changing dynamics within the Democratic Party.
The Biggest Shock of All for 2026? Pe
rhaps it’s not $6 diesel, rising interest rates, 
solid growth, booming profits or shifting politics. Rather, it’s the ability of the U.S. 
economy to absorb the unexpected, not buckle under stress, and continue generating  wealth 
for investors. To wit, the S&P 500 is on pace for its fourth
 consecutive year of double-digit 
returns.

### Page 4

4 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
2026 Has, So Far, Defied the Traditional Midterm Year Playbook
Jordy Fuentes, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Current Landscape. To no surprise, market volatility persisted in 2026. Performance initially
tracked historical midterm election patterns before diverging meaningfully as the year
progressed, though there are still three months to go. Several items remain at hand as U.S.

voters head to the polls in November, including geopolitical and foreign policy uncertainty,
energy driven inflation pressures, affordability concerns, U.S. fiscal trajectory, and rapid
advancements in AI. Regardless of the eventual political outcome, history suggests that
markets and the economy can perform well across different configurations of government.
In our view, markets continue to be anchored by strong fundamentals and encouraging
seasonal trends.

To Be Addressed. U.S. domestic and foreign policy remain in focus as the global reorder,
tit-for-tat tariffs, and geopolitical conflicts spanning the Middle East to Eastern Europe have
added layers of uncertainty to the U.S. economic outlook.
At the same time, persistent geopolitical flare-ups have pushed oil prices materially
higher, adding to inflation pressures and keeping them above the Fed’s 2% target.
Headline Consumer Price Index (CPI) held above 3% year-over-year (YoY) in August, while

its core measure, which excludes food and energy, held at 2.4% YoY. The latest Personal
Consumption Expenditure (PCE), the Fed’s preferred measure of inflation, also remained
above 3% YoY for both its headline and core figures. 2
The U.S. fiscal trajectory is another area of focus. The U.S. budget deficit stands at 5.4% of
GDP , just above this century’s average of 4.6%. Meanwhile, U.S. public sector debt surpassed
the $40 trillion mark for the first time in August, oscillating around 100% of GDP .3 While these

trends warrant close monitoring, we view them as a slow-moving structural headwind rather
than an immediate concern, particularly within a highly innovative and diverse U.S. economy.
AI has emerged as a policy consideration, with debates increasingly centered on both
regulatory safeguards and the economic implications of the expanding infrastructure buildout.
For voters, those concerns extend to affordability, as the significant energy requirements of

new data centers raise questions about potential pressure on electricity costs.
Strap In for The Ride. Leading up to the midterm election, markets tend to experience
greater swings as investors navigate uncertainty surrounding the policy path for the
remainder of the presidential cycle (Exhibit 2A). Since 1930, the S&P 500 has averaged an
intrayear drawdown of 20% in midterm years, with the latest election in 2022 experiencing
a 25% intrayear pullback. This year, however, the S&P 500 largest peak-to-trough drawdown

has been 9%, considerably shallower than its long-term average (Exhibit 2B). Yet while
volatility may rise as election day approaches, 2026 has increasingly defied the traditional
midterm year playbook.
Why 2026 is Different. Despite entering a challenging stretch of the presidential cycle,
U.S. Equities have materially outpaced the typical year two pattern (Exhibit 2C). The S&P
500 experienced its best Q2 in a midterm year since 1938, surging 14.9% compared to an

average decline of 1.6%. From a monthly perspective, September stands out as the weakest
month, though month-to-date performance points to an unusual 1.0% rise. That resilience
follows a 2.6% gain in August, another month in which performance meaningfully exceeded
the historical decline of 0.5% (Exhibit 2D).
Through September, U.S. Equity resilience has also extended beyond headline returns with
both the frequency and magnitude of market pullbacks remaining relatively subdued. Market

corrections of 10% or more have occurred in just 29% of midterm election years since 1930.4
Against this backdrop, the market’s last correction came in spring 2025, while this year has
2 Bloomberg, Bureau of Labor Statistics. Data through August 31, 2026.
3 U.S. Treasury, Bureau of Economic Analysis. Data as of September 21, 2026.
4 Bloomberg, data through December 31, 2022.
Investment Implications
In our view, any related market
weakness could represent a

potential buying opportunity for
long-term investors.

### Page 5

5 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
seen only one 5%+ pullback compared to the average three per year, underscoring relatively
resilient market conditions.5 Year-to-date, the S&P 500 has increased 13%, well above the
average annual return of 3% according to Bloomberg through September 25, and on pace
to mark the strongest midterm election year since 2006. Taken together, 2026 has thus far
defied the traditionally weak midterm year seasonal pattern, reinforcing that every election

cycle is unique and historical trends may be a useful guide to navigate market volatility.
What’s Ahead? Forward performance is increasingly constructive for Equities. In the
post-war period, the S&P 500 rose 100% of the time in the six and 12 months following
elections, averaging 13% and 14%, respectively. 6 Even narrowing the time frame to three
months, markets ended higher 90% of the time. As investors push through the finish line,
the last three months of the year tend to be the strongest with October standing out,

ending higher 67% of the time. On average, Q4 has gained 5.6% since 1930, far surpassing
the preceding three quarters of election years. 7 Greater clarity about the U.S. fiscal policy
path serves as a potential tailwind for investors following the election.
To Take Home. Volatility tends to rise during midterm years, but history suggests that
reacting to short-term political uncertainty has rarely rewarded long-term investors. Over
longer horizons, the data suggests Equity returns and economic strength are shaped less

by election outcomes than by corporate profits, valuations, labor market conditions and
capital investments.

6 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THE TICKER TAPE
What is the bond market telling us?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Office
We believe the bond market is telling us that the Fed is on track to meeting their dual
mandate of price stability and full employment. More importantly, when examining the
full yield curve the 2-year Treasury yield is currently around 4.88%, which typically is
the most sensitive to the fed funds rate and tends to price in future potential policy

adjustments. At the same time, the 10-year Treasury yield is currently about 5.11%. This
leads us to believe that the market expects the Fed to engineer a couple of more hikes
while the back end of the curve is indicating that economic growth is still healthy.
Taken together, it appears the full curve, which has clearly flattened, believes the Fed is
doing its job, while supply shocks, for obvious reasons, are not leading to higher long-run
inflation. The backup in long-dated yields is growth, not necessarily inflation as indicated

by rising “real” yields. Therefore, we view the drift higher in yields as a more normal sign
than what many have originally suggested. We continue to emphasize higher economic
growth for longer, rising productivity in the medium term, supply shortages to remain in
key areas within the AI space, and shocks in the energy equation. We expect the energy
shocks to ultimately fade away over time, but we do not believe that growth fades
anytime soon. In our view, a more normal bond market pricing in Fed moves at the short

end, and growth at the long end helps financial conditions and economic growth operate
in a more durable manner.
In summary, the front-end of the curve expects 3 to 4 more hikes in the next 18 months
or so. This can be debated obviously based on future data especially short-term energy
supply shocks. Long-end yields are drifting higher based on five main factors below, in
our view.
1. Strong economic growth (both
services and manufacturing)
2. Hawkish Fed

3. Debt and deficit concerns
4. Lower demand for long duration
sovereign debt in general
5. Energy supply shocks
The largest weight being strong economic activity which kick starts portfolio
repositioning particularly as the Fed hikes rates. The repositioning stops once demand
rises again when yields reach attractive levels for the more patient longer term
investment community. The supply shocks ultimately end and debt and deficit worries will
likely remain, but they still take a backseat for now. Longer duration government debt is

becoming more attractive each week even in the face of the concerns above. Be patient

Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

S&P 500 Index Price Return is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States. A price return index is a stock
market index that tracks only the price changes and capital gains or losses of its component securities, without including dividends or cash distributions.

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

## Document 274: France_ double-digit growth in business creations, a slowdown in the rise in bankruptcies and stable non-performing loans ratio (2026-10-08)

Source PDF: all report pdf/France_ double-digit growth in business creations, a slowdown in the rise in bankruptcies and stable non-performing loans ratio (2026-10-08).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-08
Pages: 1
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Paris, 8 October 2026

EcoFlash

France: double-digit growth in business creations, a slowdown in the rise in bankruptcies and 
stable non-performing loans ratio

Business creations rose by 12% y/y over the first eight months of 2026 1. This is significantly higher than the 5.3% y/y 
growth recorded in 2025. This increase was driven by sectors linked to information and communication technologies.

Meanwhile, business bankruptcies rose by 5.3% y/y between January and July, according to data from the Banque de 
France (compared with the 3.6% y/y increase for the whole of 2025). The rise was more pronounced in the 1st quarter (+8% y/y) 
before slowing down. This slowdown could persist, as, according to Altares, payment delays have fallen to an average of 12.3 
days in the second quarter of 2026, compared with 14.1 days a year earlier (and 13.1 days for the European average).

Rising bankruptcies are more heavily affecting the services sector, which is also experiencing slightly stronger growth 
in business creations. This correlation is particularly noticeable in the retail sector, as business creations have risen by 13% 
since the start of 2026, while bankruptcies are up by 3%. In construction and manufacturing, business bankruptcies have been 
falling since the second quarter of 2026.

The average ratio of non -performing corporate loans has remained stable at a moderately high level for the past year  
(4.0% of total loans and advances in Q2 2026). In construction, where activity is more cyclical than in other sectors, the ra tio of 
non-performing loans has reached its highest level since 2019, standing at 9.5%.

The proportion of loans that have deteriorated but are not yet non-performing (“Stage 2” according to IFRS 9 terminology) 
is stable, standing at 15.4% q/q, and remains below its all -time high (16.1%). This indicator, which helps to anticipate trends in 
non-performing loans, is relatively encouraging.

Despite a significant increase since 2021, the economic weight of bankruptcies remains, on average, below its peak in 
Q1 2025 (0.53 compared with 0.61, respectively). This indicator generally allows bankruptcies to be weighted based on the size 
of the businesses. However, the economic weight of bankrupted small businesses is at a historically high level (2.11).

Take a look at our EcoCharts on trends in business creations and bankruptcies in France and the 
associated risks. It will be updated on a quarterly basis.

Thomas Humblot 
Economiste, team: Banking economics 
thomas.humblot@bnpparibas.com

Stéphane Colliac 
Head of Advanced economies, France 
stephane.colliac@bnpparibas.com

1 For more details, see Business creations and bankruptcies in France: double-digit growth in business creations, lower growth in bankruptcies, 
and stable non-performing loans, 8 October 2026.

## Document 344: The Netherlands defies Europe’s tech trend thanks to ASML

Source PDF: all report pdf/The Netherlands defies Europe’s tech trend thanks to ASML.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-26
Pages: 27
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

National Productivity Board
2024 annual report
Labour productivity in the
market sector declined by 1.4%
in 2023.
Exporters are the most productive
firms in the Netherlands, but also
their suppliers perform above
average.
CPB - February 2025
Leon Bettendorf &
Michael Polder (Statistics Netherlands)

### Page 2

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 2 of 27 
Preface 
Productivity has shifted to the top of the political agenda in the last years. In view of the limitations to a 
further expansion of the labour supply, productivity growth is the principal way to support our future living 
standard. Unfortunately, the Netherlands, as all OECD countries, has been struggling with a declining trend in 
productivity growth during the last decades. We do not yet fully understand the causes of this global 
productivity slowdown.

CPB is part of a network of independent National Productivity Boar ds in the EU. The CPB has been a 
National Productivity Board since 2017. The main NPB tasks are monitoring and analysing productivity 
developments in the Netherlands. As in previous years, we summarise research on productivity issues in this 
Annual Report 2024.

We are preparing three publications this year. First, we participate in a multi-country project of the 
Productivity Institute (UK). The aim of this project is to identify and compare country -specific productivity 
trends and pro-productivity policies. Second, we will analyse the evidence on changing bus iness dynamics in 
the Netherlands and the consequences for firm-level productivity and innovation. Finally, we will provide the 
analysis underlying our projections of long run productivity.

Pieter Hasekamp 
Director CPB

Samenvatting 
De arbeidsproductiviteit in de marktsector daalde in 2023 met 1,4%. Sinds 2000 is de productiviteit alleen 
in 2009 nog sterker gedaald. De uitfasering van de gaswinning sinds 2014 heeft de jaarlijkse productiviteits -
groei met gemiddeld 0,3%-punt gedrukt.

We begrijpen nog niet helemaal waarom de productiviteitsgroei is afgenomen. Zoals in alle OESO-landen 
heeft Nederland last van een vertraging van de productiviteitsgroei. In een literatuuronderzoek vinden we 
verschillende verklaringen, maar de bijdrage van elke oorzaak is moeilijk te bepalen vanwege de sterke 
verbanden tussen de oorzaken.

Het productiviteitsverschil tussen de 10% meest productieve bedrijven en minder productieve bedrijven 
nam in 2021 toe. We stelden in ons vorige rapport (CPB, 2024) vast dat de productiviteitskloof tussen de 
bedrijven van de top 10% en de bedrijven van de onderste 10% sterk is toegenomen  in 2020. Uit een update van 
deze analyse blijkt dat de kloof in 2021 groot bleef en niet te wijten was aan de bijzondere omstandigheden in 
2020. Productiviteitsgroei in de economie kan voortkomen uit productiviteitsgroei binnen bestaande 
bedrijven of door activiteiten te verschuiven naar productievere bedrijven. De bijdrage van de verschuiving 
daalde in de verwerkende industrie en de dienstensector in de periode 2011-2019. De bijdrage van de 
bedrijvendynamiek bleef stabiel in de handel, terwijl deze belangrijker werd voor overige industrieën (bouw, 
energie, water en afval).

Exporteurs en hun directe toeleveranciers vormen de meest productieve groep bedrijven in Nederland.  
Uit de literatuur weten we dat exporterende bedrijven productiever zijn dan niet -exporterende bedrijven. We 
laten met nieuwe netwerkdata een duidelijk patroon zien: exporteurs zijn groter en productiever dan hun 
toeleveranciers, die op hun beurt beter presteren dan hun eigen toeleveranciers. De productiviteit van 
exporteurs en hun toeleveranciers wordt versterkt via drie mechanismen: leere ffecten, selectie en 
schaalvoordelen. Beleid dat bedrijfsnetwerken versterkt, kan bijdragen aan productiviteitsgroei.

### Page 4

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 4 of 27 
Summary

Labour productivity in the market sector declined by 1.4% in 2023. Since 2000, only 2009 saw a stronger fall 
in  productivity.. Phasing out gas extraction since 2014 depressed annual productivity growth by 0.3%-point on 
average.

We do not fully understand why the productivity growth has decreased. As in all OECD countries, the 
Netherlands is suffering from a slowdown in productivity growth. A survey of the literature provides various 
explanations, but the contribution of each cause is difficult to determine due to the strong links between the 
causes.

The productivity difference between frontier firms and non -frontier firms was growing in 2021. We 
observed that the productivity gap between the top-10% firms and the bottom-10% firms strongly increased in 
2020. An update of this analysis shows that the gap remained large in 2021. Productivity growth can originate 
from growth within existing firms or by reallocating activities to more productive firms. The contribution of 
reallocation declined in manufacturing and services during the period 2011 -2019. The contribution of business 
dynamics remained stable in trade while it became more important for other industries (construction, energy, 
wate and waste).

Exporters and their direct suppliers form the most productive group of firms in the Netherlands. From 
previous research we know that exporting firms are more productive than non-exporting firms. From new 
network data, a clear pattern emerges: exporters are larger and more productive than their suppliers, who, in 
turn, outperform their own suppliers. Productivity of exporters and their suppliers benefit through three main 
channels: learning effects, selection and economies of scale. Policies that strengthen firm networks can 
contribute to fostering productivity growth.

### Page 5

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 5 of 27 
1 Introduction 
This Annual Report summarises research on productivity developments in the Netherlands. 1 It consists of four 
parts.

In the next section we summarize the unfavourable productivity developments in 2023. We show that phasing 
out gas extraction since 2014 contributed to the low productivity growth rates in the Netherlands compared to 
other European countries.

Various explanations are found in the literature why productivity growth is slowing down. No consensus is yet 
reached on the contribution of each explanation of the slowdown. We discuss the main studies in the third 
section.

The fourth section, provided by Michael Polder (Statistics Netherlands), updates and extends the evidence on 
firm-level productivity. He documents productivity differences between firms and shows that the contribution 
of continuing firms, new firms and closing firms to productivity growth differ across industries, as well as 
between time periods.

The last section exploits new data on the Dutch production networks. 2 It is well known that exporting firms are 
more productive than an average firm. This section discusses the impact on productivity of firms that are 
suppliers to exporting firms.

1 Our Annual Report of 2023 can be found at annual report 2023. The reports of the other NPBs are collected at this EC-website. 
2 This is a translation of Freeman et al. (2024).

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 6 of 27 
2 Recent productivity developments 
Labour productivity in the market sector in 2023 was 1.4% lower than in 2022. Labour productivity is 
defined as value added per hour worked. 3 Figure 2.1a shows that productivity only declined more (with -2.2%) 
during the great recession in 2009 (CBS, 2024). The decomposition in figure 2.1b shows that the negative 
labour productivity growth in 2023 was driven by negative contributions of TFP growth (-0.8%) and capital 
deepening (-0.8%).4 The number of working hours has increased, while the capital stock reduced (due to non -
ICT capital). The long term decline in capital stock per hour since the mid 2010 -s seems to continue at an even 
faster pace. TFP growth (a measure of the efficiency with which labour and capital are used in the economy) 
turned negative in 2023. TFP grew fast in 2021 and 2022, in part through recovery from the corona pandemic 
shock in 2020

Figure 2.1 Labour productivity in de market sector declined in 2023

Source: CBS (2024); 2022 & 2023 are preliminary.

Phasing out gas extraction depressed productivity growth, starting in 2014. The mining and quarrying 
industry is very capital intensive, resulting in a high labour productivity. Labour productivity in this industry 
grew on average with 0.7% before 2014, while it fell with almost 16% per year after 2014  due to the phasing out 
of gas extraction (De Vries et al., 2024). Figure 2.2 shows that the average labour productivity growth in the 
Netherlands was one of the lowest in Europe. However, whereas the average contribution of the mining 
industry was negligible in most of the countries, it was negative ( -0.3%-point) in the Netherlands (and +0.2% 
in the US). The productivity growth of the market sector excluding mining in the Netherlands was much closer 
to the average value in Europe. Next to mining, other industries that contributed negatively to productivity 
growth in 2023 are transport and storage (-0.5%-point) and manufacturing (-0.4%-point).

estate.

capital growth, 𝐶̃ is labour composition growth, 𝐴̃ is TFP  growth and 𝛼 is the share of capital costs.

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 8 of 27 
3 Explaining the productivity 
slowdown: a survey 
We do not sufficiently understand current productivity developments, which makes insight into future 
developments uncertain. As in many other countries, the Netherlands is suffering from a slowdown in 
productivity growth. In the literature, various explanations are put forward as to why growth has decreased, 
but the contribution of each cause is difficult to determine due to the  strong links between the causes. In 
addition, it is uncertain whether the outlined trend developments are permanent or temporary. For example, 
will artificial intelligence really develop into a general purpose technology that will put an end to the weak 
productivity growth? And, if so, how long will that last?  
All OECD countries are experiencing slowdowns in productivity growth. Figure 3.1a shows the annual 
growth of labour productivity in the Netherlands in the total economy and in the market sector (this is 
excluding the government). GDP per hour worked grew on average around 2 percent around the turn of the 
century. This growth has fallen back to below 1 percent after 2009. Figure 3.1b shows the slowdown in other 
countries. The decline in GDP growth per hour worked started in all countries before the Great Recession 
(2008-2012). The Great Recession may therefore have exacerbated the slo wdown but cannot be the cause 
(Fernald et al., 2023).

The global slowdown in productivity growth suggests common causes but there is no consensus yet on 
which cause is the most important and not even on whether the slowdown will be temporary or long -
term. This slowdown puzzle contributes to the great uncertainty of projections in the near and distant future. 
We discuss the most important, global causes one by one, although they are strongly related.  5

Figure 3.1 Labour productivity growth is falling (value added per hour worked)

Source: De Vries et al. (2024), figure 3.1.; De Bondt et al. (2021), figure 3.1.1.

5 See the surveys of Moss et al. (2020), Akcigit et al. (2021) and Goldin et al. (2024). For a discussion of specific Dutch causes, such as the 
large share of self-employed people and low R&D investments, we refer to Roelandt et al. (2019) and de Bondt et al. (2021).

### Page 9

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 9 of 27 
3.1 The diminishing potential of innovative ideas 
Technology pessimists are skeptical about innovations that can still significantly increase productivity. 
Gordon (2016) argues that the number and breadth of innovations was exceptional in the period 1870 -1970 and 
that we have now returned to the normal, structural level. Bloom et al. (2020) argue that the productivity of 
researchers has fallen sharply because it is becoming increasingly difficult to find new ideas. They express the 
TFP growth as the product of the number of researchers and their productivi ty. They observe a strong increase 
in the effective number of researchers, but the problem is that productivity in research has fallen just as 
sharply. The best illustration is provided by Moore's Law. The number of transistors in an integrated circuit has  
doubled every two years, but this required an increasing input of resources.

Jones (2022) lists three reasons why growth in GDP per capita will slow down in the future. Jones analyses 
economic growth in the US with a model in which TFP growth is determined by a Research & Development 
(R&D) sector. The first reason for a slowdown in growth is that the three main determinants after 1953, apart 
from population growth, can hardly grow any further. The contribution of population growth to growth in the 
past is 20%, while 80% stems from a rising level of education, an improved allocation of production factors 
and a growing size of the R&D sector (as % of population). The problem is that the last three factors are 
naturally limited. The second reason is that a slowdown in employment growth in R&D is already observed. 
The third reason is that natural population growth (excl. migration) is already negative in many developed 
countries. In the long run, economic growth remains proportional to population growth.

Technology optimists, on the other hand, emphasise the future potential of innovation in ICT;  see for 
example Brynjolfsson and McAfee (2014). As with previous industrial revolutions, they expect that it will take 
time before the combination and diffusion of multiple new technologies will lead to a revival of productivity 
growth. Artificial Intelligence (AI) could become a new general purpose technology. AI is expected to have 
major effects on the economy, and on productivity in particular, because AI not on ly increases the productivity 
of the production of products but also the production of innovative ideas (R&D). Initial studies estimate that 
AI increases macroeconomic productivity by 1%-point per year for 10 years (see the discussion in Aldasoro et 
al., 2024). However, Acemoglu (2024) is skeptical about the extrapolation of early, simple applications and 
calculates that TFP growth increases by less than 0.1%-points per year. In addition to the potential of AI, Jones 
(2022) believes that growth can accelerate in the future because there is still sufficient room for expansion of 
the R&D sector at the global level.

Philippon (2022) disputes the existence of a structural break in technological progress. He criticizes the 
standard specification of the TFP growth rate. The literature assumes a constant TFP growth rate (i.e. 
exponential growth), while Philippon argues that the evidence is more in line with a constant absolute growth 
rate (additive growth). If absolute growth remains constant, the TFP growth rate will decline in the future. In 
the US, he finds a structural break in TFP growth around 1930 in the period 1890 -2019. Due to catch-up growth, 
TFP growth in the eurozone and Japan was higher than in the US until 1990. After 1990, absolute growth 
declined sharply and became smaller than in the US. Assuming additive growth, Philippon finds no evidence 
of a recent slowdown in technological progress.

3.2 Divergence between high- and low-productivity firms 
The OECD found that the difference in productivity between high -productivity firms and low-productivity 
firms has increased (Berlingieri et al. 2017a). Because knowledge about new production techniques and 
organizational forms is spread less quickly, low-productivity firms are less able to keep up with the

### Page 10

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 10 of 27 
productivity growth of high-productivity firms. The deterioration in knowledge diffusion m ight be related to 
the increasing importance of intangible capital (see section 3.6). The divergent productivity development may 
explain the slowdown in productivity growth.

In the Netherlands, the productivity difference between the most productive and median firms started to 
increase around 2015. De Bondt et al. (2021, Table 5.3.2) find that the difference in both labo ur productivity 
and TFP increased before 2005 during the period 2000-2015. The divergence in productivity was stronger in 
industry than in the service sector. Research in van Heuvelen et al. (2018) confirms that the productivity gap 
between the top 10% firms and the median firms has remained stable in the period 2006-2015. In the next 
section we show with a new dataset that the top-median gap increased by 0.7% annually from 2015 to 2019. 
The divergence hypothesis is not a good explanation since the slowdown in productivity growth started much 
earlier in the Netherlands.

3.3 Increase in market concentration 
According to this hypothesis, lower productivity growth is associated with the trend towards less 
competitive markets. Firms with more market power have fewer incentives to increase their productivity. The 
emergence of less competitive markets is related to the superstars hypothesis (or winner -takes-all).6 After 
superstars have acquired a dominant market position, they shift their activities from innovative investments to 
strategies to protect their excess profits. De Ridder (2024) argues that the increase in intangible capital explains 
these developments. Since intangible capital is characterized by a combination of low marginal costs and high 
fixed costs, this promotes the growth of large firms and a reduction in productivity growth. The increase in 
market power seems to be a problem mainly in the US. 7 De Loecker et al. (2020) estimate the markup of firms 
(defined as the ratio of price and marginal costs) as a measure of market power. They find that the average 
markup in the US nearly tripled from 1980 to 2016.

However, an increase in market concentration can also promote productivity growth.  When more 
productive firms gain market share at the expense of less productive firms, the reallocation contributes to 
aggregate productivity growth.8 In Europe, the increase in market concentration does not seem to be 
associated with more abuse of market power. Bighelli et al. (2023) estimate stable markups in 15 European 
countries. Van Heuvelen et al. (2021) do not find that markups in the Netherlands  increased significantly in the 
period 2006-2016. The slowdown in productivity growth in the Netherlands therefore does not seem to be 
explained by the increase in the market power of firms.

3.4 Weakening of business dynamism 
Productivity growth has been affected by reduced business dynamism. With healthy business dynamism, 
old, low-productivity firms are replaced by more productive new firms (principle of creative destruction). 
Released inputs are put to more productive use, which contributes to a stimulation of productivity growth. 
Decker et al. (2017) point to the relationship between reduced business dynamism and slowed productivity 
growth in the US. Freeman et al. (2021) show that business dynamism in the Netherlands is slo wing down 
(after 2006), mainly as a result of a declining share of start-ups. They find that the reduced dynamism has

6 See e.g. Autor et al. (2020). 
7 See e.g. Eeckhout (2022). This hypothesis is contested by Shapiro et al. (2024). 
8 See the discussion of the distinction between good and bad concentration in Covarrubias et al. (2019).

### Page 11

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 11 of 27 
contributed to lower TFP growth (by 0.2%-points per year), especially in the service sector. The weakening of 
business dynamism may be a cause, but not the main cause of the slowdown in productivity growth.

3.5 Shifts in sector structure 
The shift towards the service sector reduces average productivity growth. The share of the service sector 
(incl. healthcare) in the economy increases, while the productivity of the service sector grows less than that of 
industry. De Bondt et al. (2021, section 5.2) confirm that the shift towards the healthcare and service sectors 
has had a negative effect on productivity growth in the period 1996 -2016, but that this shift is not large enough 
to really explain the slowdown in productivity growth (see an update in De Vries et al., 2024). Most of the 
productivity growth is found within the industries, while the negative reallocation effect between sectors 
remains small. Duernecker et al. (2023) compare the strong growth in labo ur productivity in the period 1970-
1985 with the slow growth in 2002-2017 for several high-income countries. They also show that the decline in 
productivity growth in the Netherlands (2.7%-points) is only partly due to the changed sector structure (0.5%-
points).

3.6 Measurement problems 
The literature also suggests that the growth slowdown is not a real phenomenon but is a consequence of 
larger measurement problems of the new economy. Measuring productivity faces classic problems, e.g. the 
correction for quality improvements in the healthcare sector. But the problems have been exacerbated by the 
digitalization of the economy.9 For example, before we could take digital photos, the productivity of 
developing and printing photos could be calculated. Nowadays, we store and view photos on our phones. How 
should the productivity of this ‘free’ service be calculated? However, calculat ions by Syverson (2021) indicate 
that measurement problems are probably insufficient to explain the productivity slowdown.

Measurement problems are specifically mentioned in the valuation of the increasing intangible capital  
stock. Intangibles consist of, for example, R&D, software, human and organizational capital. According to 
Brynjolfsson et al. (2021), errors in measuring productivity effects of investments in intangibles can be 
described with a J-curve. At the beginning of the project, productivity is underestimated because inputs are 
also used for the production of intangibles that are poorly measured. In the course of th e project, productivity 
is overestimated because intangibles are also used for the production of measured tangibles. In the 
Netherlands, too, intangible investments now exceed tangible investments, making it more difficult to 
estimate TFP growth.

3.7 Concluding remarks 
We conclude that the literature is still very uncertain about the true causes of the slowdown in 
productivity growth and about the development of trends in the future. It is still unclear what the 
contribution of each cause is to explaining the slowdown (see the approach in Goldin et al., 2024). And what is 
the role of links between the causes? The growing importance of intangibles seems to be related to the 
increasing market power of superstars and the reduced business dynamics. A second question is how 
permanent are the outlined trend developments? Will the technology pessimists or the optimists be right 
about the breakthrough of new, substantial innovations? Is the weakening of business dynamics a temporary

9 See the recent overview by Martin et al. (2024) and also De Bondt et al. (2021, section 6).

### Page 12

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 12 of 27 
or a long-term phenomenon? Given these uncertainties, CPB, as the NPB, will continue to study the low 
productivity growth.

4 Productivity differences across 
Dutch firms are large and 
persistent 
Michael Polder (Statistics Netherlands)

We present an update and extension of evidence on the dynamics of firm-level heterogeneity in labour 
productivity, and the relation between business dynamics and aggregate (industry -level) productivity. 
Understanding more about heterogeneity and the role of dynamics is essential for understanding aggregate 
growth. In particular, statistics about the within-industry dispersion in productivity are informative about 
whether the performance of firms in a particular industry is similar, or whether there are “win ners” and 
“losers”. A stylized fact in the literature is that the latter is the case (e.g. Syverson, 2011): the degree of 
heterogeneity in productivity is persuasive, even within narrowly defined industries . This is indicative for firm -
level differences in things such as production technologies and innovation, as well as market frictions 
(Bartelsman and Wolf, 2018). In addition, productivity differences are found to be closely linked to differences 
in renumeration of workers (Cunningham et al. 2023). Finally, macroeconomic growth depends in part on 
creative destruction, through which the least productive firms are driven out of the market, making way for 
new innovative and more productive firms, as well as reallocation of market shares towards more producti ve 
incumbent firms (Aghion et al. 2021).

A decade of productivity dispersion 
Frontier firms in the Netherlands are about twice as productive as the median firm, whereas the bottom 
group is about half as productive as the median. Productivity is measured as value added per hour worked. 
Dispersion is measured by differences between percentiles of the productivity distribution.  In particular, we 
express in figure 4.1 the labour productivity level of the top decile and the bottom decile relative to the median 
level. It shows large differences between firms at the top and the bottom of the distribution.

Note on data used 
We use an experimental dataset on firm-level productivity that is currently being developed at Statistics 
Netherlands. This dataset is an updated and improved version of that used in the previous edition of the 
National Productivity Monitor (CPB, 2024). The outcomes presented should be regarded as experimental. 
In addition, due to differences in delineation of the research population, methodological choices (e.g. 
outlier exclusion), and variable definitions, these figures may not fully align with official statistics. The 
dataset currently covers the period 2011-2021 and concerns incorporated enterprises only (legal forms “bv” 
and “nv”) in the business economy (excluding the financial and real estate sectors as well as petroleum). 
In the indicators we only consider firms with 3 persons (full-time) employed or more.

### Page 13

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 13 of 27 
Figure 4.1 Labour productivity, frontier (top-10%) and laggard (bottom-10%) firms compared to median, business 
economy, 2011-2021

Note: top, median and bottom refer to respectively 90th percentile, median, and 10th percentile of the productivity distribution. 
Productivity is measured as real value added over persons employed in full-time equivalents. Percentile deviations are calculated 
within-industry (NACE Rev. 2, two digit) and year. Only enterprises with 3 or more persons employed and legal personality 
(corporations) have been included, that are part of the Business Economy (NACE Rev. 2 B-N, excl. K, incl. 95; we also exclude 19 and L). 
Nominal value added is deflated using National Accounts industry-level deflators. For more on the underlying data, see CBS (2022a, 
Chapter 2).

Growing dispersion in the Netherlands can already be detected before the Covid period. Figure 4.2 shows 
the change in dispersion relative to the base year 2011. While the figure confirms the stable pattern found in 
van Heuvelen et al. (2018) for up to 2014/2015, an increase in dispersion can be detected for more recent years.  
This trend started already before Covid. From 2015 to 2019, the top-bottom gap increased by on average 1.2% 
annually, composed of a 0.7% increase of the top-median gap and a 0.5% between median and bottom. 
Growing dispersion has been documented globally (Andrew et al., 2016), but there is variation across countries 
(Berlingieri et al., 2017) and over time (e.g. in the case of US manufacturing, Cunningham et al., 2023).

The dispersion increased strongly in 2020 and more gradually in 2021. When the economy was hit by the 
Covid-shock for the first time, this resulted in a substantial decrease in macroeconomic productivity (see figure 
2.1).10 Nevertheless, figure 4.2 shows that this impact has been uneven across the business population, 
resulting in a productivity spread much higher than in previous years and notably a higher gap for the lowest 
part of the distribution. Moving further into the Covid period, the dispersion continued to increase. The top -
bottom gap grew in 2021 with 1.8%, mainly due to the frontier firms moving away from the median (1.2%).

10 We note that employment is in full-time equivalents and refers to hours worked according to contract.

Multinational firms are strongly represented in the frontier group.  While the lion share of firms in the 
frontier group are independent small and medium enterprises (SMEs), their share is substantially lower than 
in the overall firm population (74.5% of frontier firms versus 83.6% in the population in 2021; table 4.1). 
Especially foreign multinational enterprises (MNEs) form a relatively large part of the frontier, compared to 
their population share (16.1% versus 9.3%). The post-2015 increase in dispersion has gone hand in hand with a 
slight shift within the frontier group, away from Dutch multinationals towards the other groups of firms.

We find that persistence at the frontier is substantial. The composition of the frontier group changes each 
year. The results in figure 4.3 suggest that persistence is rather high with over 80% of firms remaining at the 
frontier from year to year, or reappearing after a single year.11 This rate has been rather stable over time and 
across sectors.
